and Identitation of High-Quality Italian Extra-Virgin Olive Oils</h1></hgroup><div class="cg p">

<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Received 2021 May 19; Accepted 2021 Jul 13; Revised 2021 Jul 13; Issue date 2021 Aug 11.</p></section></div></div>
<div id="clp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none">
<div>© 2021 The Authors. Published by American
Chemical Society</div>
<p>Permits the broadest form of re-use including for commercial purposes, provided that author attribution and integrity are maintained (<a href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/</a>).</p>
<div class="p"><a href="/about/copyright/" class="usa-link">PMC Copyright notice</a></div>

<div>PMCID: PMC8389832  PMID: <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34319731/" class="usa-link">34319731</a>
</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/8d58/8389832/93bc68f84a9c/jf1c02981_0010.jpg" loading="lazy" id="ab-tgr1" height="335" width="664" alt="graphic file with name jf1c02981_0010.jpg"></p>
<p>The challenging process
of high-quality food authentication takes
advantage of highly informative chromatographic fingerprinting and
its identitation potential. In this study, the unique chemical traits
of the complex volatile fraction of extra-virgin olive oils from Italian
production are captured by comprehensive two-dimensional gas chromatography
coupled to time-of-flight mass spectrometry and explored by pattern
recognition algorithms. The consistent realignment of untargeted and
targeted features of over 73 samples, including oils obtained by different
olive cultivars (<em>n</em> = 24), harvest years (<em>n</em> = 3), and processing technologies, provides a solid foundation
for sample identification and discrimination based on production region
(<em>n</em> = 6). Through a dedicated multivariate statistics
workflow, identitation is achieved by two-level partial least-square
(PLS) regression, which highlights region diagnostic patterns accounting
between 58 and 82 of untargeted and targeted compounds, while sample
classification is performed by sequential application of soft independent
modeling for class analogy (SIMCA) models, one for each production
region. Samples are correctly classified in five of the six single-class
models, and quality parameters [i.e., sensitivity, specificity, precision,
efficiency, and area under the receiver operating characteristic curve
(AUC)] are equal to 1.00.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> comprehensive two-dimensional gas
chromatography, extra-virgin
olive oil, combined untargeted and targeted (UT) fingerprinting, identitation and authentication, geographical origin</p></section></section><section id="sec1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Introduction</h2>
<p>Olive
oil (OO) is one of the pillars of the Mediterranean diet
and represents the main source of fats in the countries of the Mediterranean
basin.<sup><a href="#ref1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref1">1</a></sup> In particular, extra-virgin olive
oil (EVOO) is recognized as the most valuable product among the edible
oils;<sup><a href="#ref2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref2">2</a></sup> it is extracted from fresh olive
fruits (<em>Olea europeae</em> L.) by mechanical
or physical technologies that preserve the composition of the lipid
fraction while limiting autoxidation reactions and alterations of
its native quality.<sup><a href="#ref3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref3">3</a></sup></p>
<p>The reason for
the increasing demand for olive oil of high quality,
i.e., EVOO (Commission of the European Communities, 1991; “EU
Food Qual. Labels”, 2021; IOC, 2015), is not only because of
its nutritional and health values, due to the presence of antioxidants
(i.e., tocopherols and phenolic compounds) and high oleic acid content,
but also because of its peculiar sensory characteristics<sup><a href="#ref2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref2">2</a></sup> strongly related to olive cultivar, pedoclimatic
conditions of the harvest region, olive ripeness, and extraction technology
(EU Food Qual. Labels, 2021).</p>
<p>In this context, any analytical
methodology capable of delineating
chemical patterns informative of the different functional variables
influencing the composition of EVOO is useful and has the potential
to support the valorization of high-quality products, facilitate sensory
quality evaluation/screenings on the basis of commercial classification,
as well as to counteract fraudulent practices.<sup><a href="#ref4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref4">4</a>−<a href="#ref7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref7">7</a></sup> In this latter context, the accurate
fingerprinting of the unsaponifiable fraction and of minor components
by comprehensive two-dimensional (2D) gas chromatography coupled to
mass spectrometry (GC × GC–MS) and/or to parallel flame
ionization detection (MS/FID) was successful in identifying admixtures
of OO with other fats and/or establish the product freshness/shelf
life.<sup><a href="#ref8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref8">8</a></sup></p>
<p>Active research in the development
of GC fingerprinting methodologies
includes also investigations on EVOO volatiles. Monodimensional (1D)-GC
fingerprinting accompanied by accurate profiling was recently applied
to validate the role of sesquiterpene hydrocarbons as geographical
origin markers<sup><a href="#ref7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref7">7</a></sup> in EVOOs from different
cultivars and production areas. In their study, Quintanilla-Casas
et al.<sup><a href="#ref7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref7">7</a></sup> confirmed the superior discrimination
power of the total volatile fingerprint (100% correct classification)
obtained by GC–MS raw data processing followed by suitable
supervised chemometrics, compared to the targeted profiling of selected
sesquiterpenoids, whose correctness ranged between 46 and 100% of
the correct classification, as a function of the production country.</p>
<p>Moreover, using volatile fingerprinting based on 1D-GC, it was
also possible to support the commercial classification of OO based
on sensory panel evaluation.<sup><a href="#ref9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref9">9</a></sup> EVOOs are
in fact characterized by peculiar yet essential aroma qualities such
as green, grassy, and fruity notes, whose perception is at the basis
of commercial classification based on EU regulations. Sensory quality
in fact, together with compositional/chemical standards to be complied,<sup><a href="#ref5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref5">5</a></sup> and absence of off-flavors guide the OO classification
in EVOO (median of the defects = 0.0 and median of the positive attribute
&gt; 0.0), virgin olive oil (VOO—median of the defects between
0.0 and 3.5 and median of the positive attribute &gt; 0.0), and lampante
olive oil (LOO—median of the defects &gt; 6.0) in the presence
of sensory defects (rancid, fusty/muddy, musty, and winey/vinegary),
even at lower levels (e.g., median of the defects ≠ 0).<sup><a href="#ref5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref5">5</a></sup></p>
<p>In this study, we take a step forward in
the direction of validating
a powerful and highly flexible chromatographic fingerprinting workflow
with superior identitation (i.e., defining the identity of a particular
food based on the characteristic features that make it singular or
unique<sup><a href="#ref10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref10">10</a></sup>) and classification effectiveness
compared to existing tools based on 1D-GC data. The improved separation
capacity of GC × GC, the analyte retention logic over the separation
space,<sup><a href="#ref11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref11">11</a></sup> and the comprehensive capture
of a component’s features generated by time-of-flight mass
spectrometry (TOF MS) detection make the resulting 2D fingerprints
the sample’s unique traits for effective and reliable authentication.
Moreover, the specificity of the third information dimension of the
system (i.e., EI-MS fragmentation patterns) gives access to a higher
informative level as any confirmatory analytical technique.</p>
<p>Compared to existing studies adopting GC × GC as the profiling
and/or fingerprinting technique,<sup><a href="#ref12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref12">12</a>−<a href="#ref14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref14">14</a></sup> the combined information
derived from untargeted and targeted (UT) features is here explored
in the challenging scenario of Italian high-quality EVOO production
connoted by an impressive heritage of olive genetic varieties, with
about 540 different registered cultivars<sup><a href="#ref15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref15">15</a></sup> and 46 protected designation of origin (PDO) products from different
geographical locations (i.e., regions) over the entire territory.</p>
<p>The challenge posed by the complexity and high chemical dimensionality
of EVOO volatiles is tackled by a dedicated workflow, named combined
untargeted and targeted fingerprinting (UT fingerprinting),<sup><a href="#ref16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref16">16</a></sup> where the information from known and unknown
components patterns are accurately tracked across many samples and
their identitation, discrimination, and classification power is examined
in great detail with a focus on regional characters. Furthermore,
the synergy between profiling and fingerprinting is also examined
by observing the distribution of key-aroma compounds and potent odorants
strongly correlated to positive and/or negative odor qualities.<sup><a href="#ref17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref17">17</a>−<a href="#ref19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref19">19</a></sup></p></section><section id="sec2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Materials and Methods</h2>
<section id="sec2.1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Chemicals</h3>
<p>Pure
reference standards of α- and
β-thujone and methyl-2-octynoate used as internal standards
(ISs), <em>n</em>-alkanes (from <em>n</em>-C7 to <em>n</em>-C25) used for linear retention index (<em>I</em><sup>T</sup>) calibration, and pure reference compounds for identity
confirmation were supplied by Merck (Milan, Italy). Cyclohexane (HPLC
grade) for <em>n</em>-alkane dilution and pure dibutyl phthalate
used to prepare IS working solutions were also from Merck (Milan,
Italy).</p></section><section id="sec2.2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Extra-Virgin Olive Oil Samples</h3>
<p>Extra-virgin olive oils
(EVOOs) were supplied within the VIOLIN project<sup><a href="#ref20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref20">20</a></sup> selection. They were obtained from olives of different
cultivars harvested between 2016 and 2018 over the Italian territory;
all samples were certified as EVOOs by accredited laboratories (ISO
17025:2018) and by the official sensory panel test. Details on the
sample set under study, counting 73 samples, are provided in the Supporting
Information <a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.jafc.1c02981/suppl_file/jf1c02981_si_001.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Table S1</a> together with harvest/production
regions (i.e., Umbria <em>n</em> = 7, Garda lake <em>n</em> = 10, Lazio <em>n</em> = 11, Puglia <em>n</em> =
12, Sicilia <em>n</em> = 13, and Toscana <em>n</em> = 20). Supporting Information <a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.jafc.1c02981/suppl_file/jf1c02981_si_001.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Figure S1</a> shows geographical locations of selected EVOO production sites.</p></section><section id="sec2.3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Headspace (HS) Solid-Phase Microextraction (SPME) Devices and
Sampling Conditions</h3>
<p>Volatiles were sampled by headspace (HS)
solid-phase microextraction (SPME). A divinylbenzene/carboxen/polydimethylsiloxane
(DVB/CAR/PDMS) <em>d</em><sub>f</sub> 50/30 μm 2 cm
length fiber (Supelco, Bellefonte, PA) was chosen based on its sampling
effectiveness on EVOO volatiles and previous research.<sup><a href="#ref17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref17">17</a>,<a href="#ref21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref21">21</a>−<a href="#ref23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref23">23</a></sup> SPME fibers were conditioned before use as recommended
by the manufacturer.</p>
<p>The ISs were preloaded onto the SPME device
by sampling 5.0 μL of α/β-thujone and methyl-2-octynoate
IS solution (100 mg L<sup>–1</sup>) placed in a 20 mL headspace
vial. IS preloading was performed by exposing the SPME device to the
HS kept at 40 °C for 5 min.</p>
<p>Sampling was carried out on
0.100 ± 0.005 g of oil samples,
precisely weighed in 20 mL headspace vials, at 40 °C for 60 min
under constant stirring. The amount of sample was chosen matching
for HS linearity conditions for most of the characteristic analytes
of the EVOO volatile fraction.<sup><a href="#ref21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref21">21</a>,<a href="#ref24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref24">24</a>,<a href="#ref25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref25">25</a></sup> After extraction, the SPME device was automatically transferred
to the split/splitless injection port of the GC × GC system kept
at 250 °C, and thermal desorption was for 5 min.</p></section><section id="sec2.4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">GC × GC-TOF
MS: Instrument Setup and Conditions</h3>
<p>GC × GC analyses
were performed on an Agilent 7890B GC unit
(Agilent Technologies, Wilmington DE) coupled to a Markes BenchTOF-Select
mass spectrometer featuring tandem ionization (Markes International,
Llantrisant, U.K.). The GC transfer line was set at 270 °C. TOF
MS tuning parameters were set for single ionization at 70 eV, and
the scan range was set at 40–350 <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> with a spectra acquisition frequency of 100 Hz. The system
was equipped with a two-stage KT 2004 loop-type thermal modulator
(Zoex Corporation, Houston, TX) cooled with liquid nitrogen and controlled
by Optimode v2.0 (SRA Intruments, Cernusco sul Naviglio, Milan, Italy).
Modulation period (<em>P</em><sub>M</sub>) and hot jet pulse
times were set, respectively, at 3.5 s and 300 ms, with a cold jet
stream at the mass flow controller (MFC) from 40 to 8% of the total
flow along the run duration. No secondary oven was adopted in the
GC × GC setup.</p></section><section id="sec2.5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">GC × GC Columns and Settings</h3>
<p>The column set was
configured as follows: <sup>1</sup>D DB-HeavyWax column (100% polyethylene
glycol; 30 m × 0.25 mm <em>d</em><sub>c</sub> ×
0.25 μm <em>d</em><sub>f</sub>) from Agilent J&amp;W
(Wilmington, DE) coupled with a <sup>2</sup>D OV1701 column (86% polydimethylsiloxane,
7% phenyl, 7% cyanopropyl; 1 m × 0.1 mm <em>d</em><sub>c</sub> × 0.10 μm <em>d</em><sub>f</sub>) from
Agilent Technologies (Wilmington, DE). A fused silica capillary loop
(1.0 m × 0.1 mm <em>d</em><sub>c</sub>) was used in the
modulator slit.</p>
<p>The GC split/splitless injector port was kept
at 250 °C and operated in the split mode with a split ratio of
1:20. The carrier gas was helium at a constant nominal flow of 1.3
mL min<sup>–1</sup>. The oven temperature programming was set
as follows: from 40 °C (2 min) to 240 °C (10 min) at 3.5
°C min<sup>–1</sup>.</p>
<p>The <em>n</em>-alkane
solution for <em>I</em><sup>T</sup> determination was analyzed
under the following conditions:
split/splitless injector in the split mode, split ratio 1:50, injector
temperature 250 °C, and injection volume 1 μL.</p></section><section id="sec2.6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Combined Untargeted
and Targeted (UT) Fingerprinting Workflow</h3>
<p>The data processing
workflow was designed to comprehensively capture
the chemical signature of volatiles from EVOO samples by computing
both peak and peak-region features from untargeted (unknowns) and
targeted components located over the 2D space. The approach, named
UT fingerprinting, was designed on EVOO volatile patterns and further
adapted to compositional peculiarities of samples in other fields.<sup><a href="#ref11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref11">11</a></sup> In this study, the targeting (i.e., identification)
of analytes was done as the last step of the process after chromatogram
realignment over reliable peaks from untargeted components/features.</p>
<p>The generation of untargeted features (i.e., peaks and peak regions)
and their realignment across all sample chromatograms were performed
by template matching<sup><a href="#ref26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref26">26</a></sup> and actively uses
metadata, collected for 2D peaks and peak regions (i.e., retention
times, MS spectrum, and detector response) above a signal-to-noise
(S/N) threshold value of 100,<sup><a href="#ref23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref23">23</a></sup> to establish
correspondences across 2D patterns. Realignment specificity is done
by active constraints on MS similarity [i.e., a threshold value of
750 for direct match factor DMF and reverse match factor RMF according
to the NIST MS Search algorithm, ver. 2.0 (National Institute of Standards
and Technology, Gaithersburg, MD)] between template (reference) and
candidate (analyzed) “peak spectra”.<sup><a href="#ref23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref23">23</a>,<a href="#ref27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref27">27</a>−<a href="#ref29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref29">29</a></sup></p>
<p>The chromatographic fingerprinting was performed
automatically
by the GC Image Investigator V2.9 (GC Image LLC, Lincoln NE) on a
random selection of sample chromatograms (<em>n</em> = 25)
acquired across a time-frame of 2 weeks. It aligned the 25 chromatograms
through reliable peaks for registration and generated a composite
chromatogram over which peak-region features were delineated and extracted
to form a feature template for further processing. Reliable peaks
in this study were those that positively matched across all but one
of the selected 25 chromatograms (i.e., most constrained condition
option).</p>
<p>The resulting feature template includes untargeted
(reliable) peaks
and peak regions comprehensively capturing the chemical composition
of samples. <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>A shows the pseudocolor image of a Sicilian EVOO (#S1) overlaid with
591 peak regions (red graphics) and 159 targeted peaks (green circles).
Targeting of informative compounds, including EVOO key-aroma compounds,
ripening indicators, and potent odorants responsible for coded defects,<sup><a href="#ref30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref30">30</a></sup> was performed at the end of the realignment
process over the entire set of chromatograms (<em>n</em> =
73). Identifications were confirmed by authentic standards when available
in the authors’ laboratory (criterion “a” in <a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Table <a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>) or by spectral similarity
DMF ≥ 900, RMF ≥ 950, and <em>I</em><sup>T</sup> tolerance ± 20 units (criterion “b”, corresponding
to tentative identification in <a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Table <a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>). <a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Table <a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">1</a></a> lists target analytes with <sup>1</sup>D and <sup>2</sup>D retention
times (<sup>1</sup><em>t</em><sub>R</sub>; <sup>2</sup><em>t</em><sub>R</sub>), precision data (see the Method Performance
Parameters section), experimental (Exp.) and tabulated (Lit.) <sup>1</sup>D <em>I</em><sup>T</sup> values, and odor descriptors
as reported in the reference literature.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig1"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=8389832_jf1c02981_0002.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/8d58/8389832/ace382932032/jf1c02981_0002.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr1" height="1029" width="762" alt="Figure 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Pseudocolor image (A)
of a Sicilian EVOO (Sicilia origin—ID#S1)
volatile fraction comprehensively mapped through untargeted and targeted
(UT) peak regions (red graphics); identified/targeted analytes (i.e.,
targeted compounds) are highlighted by green circles. (B) Patterns
of analytes, following a retention logic based on the relative retention
exerted by the polar × semipolar column combination adopted (alkanes,
cyano; alkenes, blue; saturated, brown aldehydes; unsaturated, orange
aldehydes; alcohols, purple; terpenoids, gray; and fatty acids, black),
are highlighted. (C) Enlarged area of lipoxygenase (LOX) derivatives
(red circles).</p></figcaption></figure><section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="tbl1"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 1. List of 159 Target
Peaks, Together
with Retention Times in the Two Analytical Dimensions (<sup>1</sup><em>t</em><sub>R</sub>, <sup>2</sup><em>t</em><sub>R</sub>), % Relative Standard Deviation (% RSD) Calculated over Six
Analytical Replicates over 2 Weeks and Referred to Retention Times
and 2D Peak Volumes; Experimental <sup>1</sup>D <em>I</em><sup>T</sup> and Tabulated <em>I</em><sup>T</sup> Values;

<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="t1fn1">
<sup>a</sup><p class="display-inline">Odor descriptors as reported in
the reference literature.<sup><a href="#ref17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref17">17</a>,<a href="#ref19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref19">19</a>,<a href="#ref36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref36">36</a>,<a href="#ref37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref37">37</a>,<a href="#ref44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref44">44</a></sup></p>
</div></div></section><p>The output table collecting
2D peaks and peak regions aligned across
all chromatograms with feature-related metadata (<sup>1</sup>D and <sup>2</sup>D retention times, MS spectrum, base peak and molecular ion <em>m</em>/<em>z</em>, and TIC response) was stored and made
available for further processing.</p>
<p>Supporting Information <a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.jafc.1c02981/suppl_file/jf1c02981_si_001.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Table S2</a> lists
untargeted and targeted peak-region features included in the UT template,
together with their experimental <sup>1</sup>D <em>I</em><sup>T</sup> values, retention times in the two analytical dimensions
(<sup>1</sup><em>t</em><sub>R</sub>, <sup>2</sup><em>t</em><sub>R</sub>), % relative standard deviation (% RSD) on retention
times across all analyses, and reference MS spectral signature from
the peak-apex spectrum.</p></section><section id="sec2.7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Method Performance Parameters</h3>
<p>Repeatability
was evaluated
on analytical descriptors considered fundamental for an accurate chromatographic
fingerprinting based on both 2D peak patterns and analyte responses.
Therefore, % RSD was calculated on retention times and analyte % response
(% normalized 2D volumes over IS) for all targeted compounds and on
analytical replicates of the same sample analyzed every 2 days over
the 2 weeks of the study (<em>n</em> = 6). Results are reported
in <a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Table <a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>. Mean %
RSDs on retention times were 0.34% for <sup>1</sup>D (<sup>1</sup><em>t</em><sub>R</sub>) and 3.01% for <sup>2</sup>D (<sup>2</sup><em>t</em><sub>R</sub>). Maximum % RSD on percent
response was instead 20.93%, reported for nonanal, while the mean
value was 11.98%.</p></section><section id="sec2.8"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Data Acquisition and 2D Data Processing</h3>
<p>Data were acquired
by TOF-DS software (Markes International, Llantrisant, U.K.) and processed
by the GC Image V2.9 suite (GC Image, LLC Lincoln, NE).</p>
<p>The
data files of peak-region features from each chromatogram were exported
in the “.xls” format (Microsoft Excel) and then converted
to the MATLAB format (version R2017b). All of the multivariate analyses
were performed using PLS_Toolbox 8.6.1 (Eigenvector Research, Manson,
WA) for the MATLAB environment (MathWorks Inc., Massachusetts, R2017b).
Principal component analysis (PCA), partial least-squares regression
(PLS), and soft independent modeling for class analogy (SIMCA) were
applied as exploratory analysis, variable selection, and classification
method, respectively. In addition, data were preprocessed by autoscaling
before model development. Microsoft Excel spreadsheet was used for
similarity analysis.</p></section></section><section id="sec3"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Results and Discussion</h2>
<p>Chromatographic
fingerprinting based on comprehensive two-dimensional
separations has a great potential for discrimination and identification
of samples based on their chemical signatures, a process described
as identitation.<sup><a href="#ref10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref10">10</a></sup> Moreover, it offers
further advantages when mass spectrometry is used at the detection
level, providing additional information for analyte putative identification.
This step gives access to a higher information level on sample properties
and characteristics.<sup><a href="#ref7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref7">7</a>,<a href="#ref18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref18">18</a>,<a href="#ref31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref31">31</a></sup></p>
<p>The strategy adopted to decrypt the hidden information from
volatile
patterns of EVOOs harvested in different Italian regions collects
information from untargeted and targeted (UT) features. It is a fingerprinting
approach designed to comprehensively map all detectable volatiles
from GC × GC-TOF MS analyses.<sup><a href="#ref16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref16">16</a></sup> Chromatogram
processing was done by a validated workflow described in the Combined
Untargeted and Targeted (UT) Fingerprinting Workflow section; the
output was a data matrix of dimensions 73 × 519 (i.e., samples
× features) with a subset of 159 identified (targeted) compounds.</p>
<p>The next section highlights the fundamental role of high-resolution
separations and retention pattern logic based on effective identitation
of samples. Machine learning, based on multivariate statistics and
modeling algorithms, will be presented as a key tool to access a higher
level of information to identify distinctive regional marker patterns.</p>
<section id="sec3.1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Complex
and Multidimensional EVOO Volatilome</h3>
<p>EVOO is
highly appreciated by consumers because of its unique and characteristic
flavor, which reflects the chemical complexity and dimensionality<sup><a href="#ref32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref32">32</a></sup> of its volatile fraction, characterized by the
presence of many compounds, especially carbonyls (e.g., aldehydes,
ketones), esters, alcohols, and hydrocarbons (e.g., linear, aromatic,
terpenoids, etc.). Odor-active compounds, with a low odor perception
threshold, and volatiles lacking sensory features (i.e., interferents),<sup><a href="#ref33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref33">33</a></sup> concur in the modulation of the “odor
code” while triggering aroma perception, whose objectification
by instrumental methods is challenging.<sup><a href="#ref9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref9">9</a>,<a href="#ref34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref34">34</a></sup> However, EVOO
volatiles encrypt additional information about relevant functional
variables including olive cultivars, the olive tree’s harvest
region and local pedoclimatic conditions, olive ripeness, technological
processes, and storage condition.<sup><a href="#ref1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref1">1</a>,<a href="#ref2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref2">2</a>,<a href="#ref35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref35">35</a>,<a href="#ref36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref36">36</a></sup></p>
<p><a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>A shows the pseudocolor image
of a Sicilian EVOO (Sicilia origin—#S1) volatile fraction comprehensively
mapped through untargeted and targeted (UT) peak regions (red graphics);
identified/targeted analytes (i.e., targeted compounds) are highlighted
by green circles. Patterns of analytes, following a retention logic
based on the relative retention exerted by the polar × semipolar
column combination adopted, are highlighted in <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>B,C.</p>
<p>Compounds derived from oxidative
cleavage of linoleic and linolenic
acids, promoted by lipoxygenase (LOX) and hydroperoxide lyase (HPL)
pathways, constitute the LOX signature (green-color area in <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>B and enlarged area
in <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>C), which
is the most abundant fraction in high-quality EVOOs.<sup><a href="#ref1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref1">1</a>,<a href="#ref13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref13">13</a></sup> It is characterized by the presence of C<sub>6</sub> and C<sub>5</sub> compounds, in particular aldehydes, alcohols, ketones, and esters
(e.g., hexanal, (<em>E</em>)-2-hexenal, 1-penten-3-ol, 1-hexanol,
1-penten-3-one, hexyl acetate, etc.), fundamental to define positive
attributes as fruity and green.<sup><a href="#ref14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref14">14</a>,<a href="#ref21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref21">21</a></sup></p>
<p>Saturated and
unsaturated aldehydes (respectively, in brown and
orange) are mainly produced by oxidation of unsaturated fatty acids.<sup><a href="#ref37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref37">37</a></sup> While C<sub>6</sub> and C<sub>5</sub> unsaturated
aldehydes from LOX are correlated to positive attributes, the others,
with a higher molecular weight and low odor threshold (e.g., (<em>E</em>)-2-heptenal, (<em>E</em>)-2-octenal, (<em>E</em>)-2-decenal, heptanal, octanal, and nonanal), are indicated
in many studies as responsible for the rancid off-flavor with unpleasant
and penetrating notes.<sup><a href="#ref19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref19">19</a>,<a href="#ref37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref37">37</a>−<a href="#ref39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref39">39</a></sup> Alcohols (purple
line in <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>B),
represented by 30 congeners here identified, have a strong retention
in the <sup>1</sup>D polar column and are well separated by informative
carbonyls. Of them, the most relevant are 1-octen-3-ol, 1-nonanol,
and 1-decanol because of their decisive role in defining sensory defects
eliciting fatty, rancid, earthy, and mushroom-like notes.<sup><a href="#ref24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref24">24</a>,<a href="#ref36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref36">36</a></sup></p>
<p>Short-chain fatty acids (<a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>B black line) derive from the oxidation of
the corresponding
aldehydes<sup><a href="#ref19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref19">19</a>,<a href="#ref37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref37">37</a></sup> with propanoic and butanoic acids as the
most odor-active, followed by pentanoic and heptanoic acids. Their
presence was correlated to the perception of rancid and fusty defects.<sup><a href="#ref1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref1">1</a>,<a href="#ref37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref37">37</a>,<a href="#ref38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref38">38</a></sup></p>
<p>Hydrocarbons (<a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>B in cyano) have
a negligible contribution in the definition of the
EVOO flavor, although some unsaturated derivatives (i.e., 3-ethyl-1,5-octadiene
and 4,8-dimethyl-1,3,7-nonatriene) were linked to green and fruity
notes<sup><a href="#ref13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref13">13</a></sup> or to rancid and fishy aroma.<sup><a href="#ref1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref1">1</a></sup> Moreover, a series of C10 alkenes, i.e., 3,4-diethyl-1,5-hexadiene
(<em>RS</em> or <em>SR</em>), 3,4-diethyl-1,5-hexadiene
(meso), (5<em>Z</em>)-3-ethyl-1,5-octadiene, (5<em>E</em>)-3-ethyl-1,5-octadiene, (<em>E</em>,<em>Z</em>)-3,7-decadiene,
and (<em>E</em>,<em>E</em>)-3,7-decadiene, whose elution
region is highlighted in blue in <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>B, are known to be diagnostic markers of
early ripening stages of olives (Angerosa, Camera, D’Alessandro,
&amp; Mellerio, 1998), while <em>n</em>-octane is an indicator
of over-ripening.<sup><a href="#ref3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref3">3</a>,<a href="#ref16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref16">16</a>,<a href="#ref24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref24">24</a></sup></p>
<p>The presence and abundance of terpenes (gray rectangles in <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>B) are of particular
interest because of their role as indicators of geographical origin<sup><a href="#ref7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref7">7</a>,<a href="#ref14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref14">14</a></sup> or of ripening, e.g., α-farnesene.<sup><a href="#ref3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref3">3</a></sup> Moreover, they contribute to defining positive attributes, such
as wood, lemon, and roselike odors.<sup><a href="#ref13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref13">13</a>,<a href="#ref40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref40">40</a></sup></p>
<p>Lactones
are generally detected in low but variable amounts in
EVOO, and their relative concentration is cultivar-specific.<sup><a href="#ref36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref36">36</a></sup> Esters as well, closely eluting to lactones,
contribute to defining fruity notes, with C<sub>6</sub> and C<sub>5</sub> derivatives deriving from the LOX pathway that dominates
the class.<sup><a href="#ref1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref1">1</a>,<a href="#ref21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref21">21</a>,<a href="#ref36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref36">36</a></sup></p></section><section id="sec3.2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Multivariate
Analysis</h3>
<p>First, an exploratory unsupervised
analysis was carried out applying PCA; data structure was examined
to check whether geographical-origin-related intrinsic groupings of
olive oil samples were detectable. Then, six two-level PLS regression
models (one for each concerned Italian region) were built to obtain
the variable importance in prediction (VIP) scores and to select the
variables that contribute the most to characterize each EVOO belonging
to a particular Italian region against the rest of the samples. From
these PLS models, the six characteristic volatile patterns, one for
each geographical region, were delineated and a similarity analysis
of the characteristic pattern of each region was carried out by applying
the nearness index. Finally, six one-input class SIMCA classification
models were developed and validated. <a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">2</a></a> shows the multivariate analysis workflow
designed to capture informative and diagnostic patterns capable of
correctly classifying/discriminating EVOO production regions.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig2"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 2.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=8389832_jf1c02981_0003.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/8d58/8389832/0e26d59471a3/jf1c02981_0003.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr2" height="685" width="750" alt="Figure 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Workflow including
data processing (i.e., fingerprinting and profiling)
and machine learning.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec3.3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Exploratory Analysis</h3>
<p>PCA was initially performed considering
the 591 variables (i.e., peak-region features) per sample (<em>n</em> = 73). After inferring from this first PCA model, three
variables were removed: phthalide, (<em>E</em>)-2-hexenal,
and toluene because the related loadings were very large in all cases,
and they were masking the behavior of the other variables. Finally,
a new PCA with 588 variables was developed and all of the successive
multivariate analysis steps were carried out with these variables.</p>
<p>The new PCA model was built with 12 principal components, which
explained a total variance of 79.73%. <a href="#fig3" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig3" class="usa-link">3</a></a> displays the score plot on PC1 vs PC2. Some
particular grouping trends were observed for the olive oil samples
from Sicilia, Lazio, and Umbria. In addition, Garda and Puglia were
spread over the bottom and top halves of PC1. Notice that the variance
explained by both PCs is approximately 30% of the total variability.
This implies that the main source of variability in the data is not
related to geographical origin. Nevertheless, it is sufficient to
propose classificatory models.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig3"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 3.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=8389832_jf1c02981_0004.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/8d58/8389832/8e9ca7cb83e4/jf1c02981_0004.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr3" height="455" width="700" alt="Figure 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Principal component analysis (PCA) score
plot on PC1 vs PC2 accounting
for 79.73% of the total explained variance. The PCA model is based
on the TIC % response from UT peak regions comprehensively covering
the chromatographic space. EVOOs from different Italian regions are
displayed in different colors.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec3.4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Variable Selection: Characteristic Profile</h3>
<p>The variable
importance in the projection (VIP) score, which summarizes the overall
contribution of each variable to the PLS model, was used as the variable
selection strategy to highlight characteristic volatile patterns for
each region. The “greater-than-one-rule” was applied
for selecting the VIP scores, and only about 12% of the total number
of variables (588) were selected as characteristics. In this way,
the number of selected variables per region was Garda, 76; Sicilia,
58; Toscana, 71; Lazio, 82; Puglia, 71; and Umbria, 70; accounting
for a total of 121 variables. <a href="#tbl2" class="usa-link">Table <a href="#tbl2" class="usa-link">2</a></a> shows the numbers of LVs chosen as well as the percentage
of variance explained for each model.</p>

region, the specific variables and include both untargeted and targeted
components.</p></section><section id="sec3.5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Similarity Study</h3>
<p>The similarity
analysis among the
region characteristic patterns was carried out by calculating similarity
indices. Such indices are defined as a number between 0 and 1, which
describes the equivalence of two objects characterized by multivariate
data; the value 0 indicates maximum difference and 1 implies maximum
similarity. In this study, the nearness index (NEAR)<sup><a href="#ref41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref41">41</a></sup> was employed; it can be calculated by <a href="#eq1" class="usa-link">eq <a href="#eq1" class="usa-link">1</a></a></p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="eq1"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/8d58/8389832/6fbf0d3dc5bc/jf1c02981_m001.jpg" loading="lazy" height="49" width="209" alt="graphic file with name jf1c02981_m001.jpg"></td>
<td class="label">1</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where <em>x</em><sub>c<em><sub>i</sub></em></sub> and <em>x</em><sub>r<em><sub>i</sub></em></sub> symbolize
each element of the considered and reference
characteristic profiles, respectively. Note that <a href="#eq1" class="usa-link">eq <a href="#eq1" class="usa-link">1</a></a> has two terms. The second term is a quotient
between the sum of distances between the different elements of the
two vectors (global distance) and the value of the sum of these elements.
In this way, a normalized global distance between 0 and 1 is calculated.
This second term is subtracted from 1 to convert the distance (which
measures dissimilarity) into similarity so that 1 represents the total
coincidence and 0 represents the null coincidence.</p>
<p><a href="#eq1" class="usa-link">Equation <a href="#eq1" class="usa-link">1</a></a> could also be reformulated
in the matrix notation as reported in <a href="#eq2" class="usa-link">eq <a href="#eq2" class="usa-link">2</a></a></p>
<table class="disp-formula p" id="eq2"><tr>
<td class="formula"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/8d58/8389832/161cd2d9bb32/jf1c02981_m002.jpg" loading="lazy" height="45" width="289" alt="graphic file with name jf1c02981_m002.jpg"></td>
<td class="label">2</td>
</tr></table>
<p>where, correspondingly, <strong>X</strong><sub>c<em><sub>i</sub></em></sub> and <strong>X</strong><sub>R<em><sub>i</sub></em></sub> are the considered and reference characteristic
profile vectors, respectively (the superscript T denotes the transposed
matrix).</p>
<p>To carry out the similarity study, a new reduced tertiary
vector
consisting of 0, 1, and 2 codes for each region was built from the
regional characteristic profiles; results are reported in <a href="#tbl3" class="usa-link">Table <a href="#tbl3" class="usa-link">3</a></a>. The following rules
were applied to establish the aforementioned codes:</p>
<ul class="list" style="list-style-type:disc">
<li><p>0: It was assigned to those variables
not selected as
part of the regional characteristic pattern, e.g., variable 32 corresponding
to methyl benzoate was selected only for the Puglia profile, and thus,
this variable was codified with the value 0 for the remaining reduced
tertiary vectors.</p></li>
<li><p>1: It was assigned
to those variables whose VIP scores
ranged from 0 to 1, e.g., variable 46 corresponding to ethyl benzoate
was selected for Lazio, Puglia, and Umbria profiles.</p></li>
<li><p>2: It was assigned to those variables whose VIP scores
were greater than 1, e.g., variable 8 corresponding to α-copaene
had a VIP score greater than 1 for Garda, Sicilia, Toscana, Lazio,
and Puglia profiles and a VIP score between 0 and 1 for the Umbria
profile. Thus, in the five patterns (Garda, Sicilia, Toscana, Lazio,
and Puglia), this variable was codified with value 2 and in Umbria
having value 1.</p></li>
</ul>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="tbl3"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 3. Reduced
Tertiary Vector from Six Studied
Regions from Italy.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=8389832_jf1c02981_0006.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/8d58/8389832/7700f598e0c2/jf1c02981_0006.jpg" loading="lazy" id="fx1" height="1640" width="827" alt="graphic file with name jf1c02981_0006.jpg"></a></p>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=8389832_jf1c02981_0007.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/8d58/8389832/78b79c4415ca/jf1c02981_0007.jpg" loading="lazy" id="fx2" height="1682" width="827" alt="graphic file with name jf1c02981_0007.jpg"></a></p>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=8389832_jf1c02981_0008.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/8d58/8389832/0b3642b3db02/jf1c02981_0008.jpg" loading="lazy" id="fx3" height="1695" width="827" alt="graphic file with name jf1c02981_0008.jpg"></a></p>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=8389832_jf1c02981_0009.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/8d58/8389832/6b2cff81bd96/jf1c02981_0009.jpg" loading="lazy" id="fx4" height="223" width="827" alt="graphic file with name jf1c02981_0009.jpg"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/tbl3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p>Once the reduced
tertiary vectors from characteristic patterns
were pairwise compared, a similarity matrix was constructed from the
found NEAR values, which is shown in <a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">4</a></a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig4"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=8389832_jf1c02981_0005.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/8d58/8389832/4ce35b9e9041/jf1c02981_0005.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr4" height="396" width="627" alt="Figure 4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig4/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Similarity matrix based on the NEAR index resulting
from pairwise
comparison of regional patterns.</p></figcaption></figure><p>As can be seen in the similarity matrix, in all cases, the NEAR
value is significantly less than 1, indicating that the volatile profile/pattern
between the regions is significantly dissimilar. Therefore, it may
be used to classify samples according to geographical origin. In addition,
there were five variables out of the 121 selected that were present
in all characteristic patterns having a code higher than 1. It was
therefore decided to remove them from the classification models as
their contribution to the discrimination among regions would not be
relevant.</p></section><section id="sec3.6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Classification According to Harvest/Production Region Unique
Signature</h3>
<p>The most conventional way to develop a classification
model is based on building a model with two input classes, the target
class and the nontarget class, but a valid alternative is performing
the same classification method by training with a single input class,
i.e., the target class.<sup><a href="#ref42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref42">42</a></sup> Working with
one input class classification has significant advantages in food
authentication: the model is trained using the data from representative
samples from genuine foods (target class) and no other samples are
required. In fact, some authors have stated that it is advisable to
develop models using a one-class classifier in the case of food authentication.
Indeed, if a well-known discriminant method such as partial least
squares-discriminant analysis (PLS-DA) is used and a new sample does
not belong to any such class, the discriminant analysis is unable
to properly define the belonging of the sample to one particular class.
Conversely, a one-class classifier such as SIMCA establishes if the
acceptance is around the target class, delimiting the target samples
from other classes.<sup><a href="#ref43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref43">43</a></sup></p>
<p>SIMCA involves
building a classification method in which each class of training set
is modeled independently and the assignment of an unknown sample as
belonging to a specific class is based on the nearest distance to
the corresponding regions established in the space of principal components.
Six one-input class SIMCA models were built, one for each Italian
region. Each individual model was developed using the 116 untargeted/targeted
features, which were selected in at least one of the regional characteristic
patterns. The aim was to generate overall models suitable for application
in routine analysis. Otherwise, should it be required to classify
a sample of unknown origin, whose characteristic variables would be
selected or chosen? In this way, any classification model developed
can be applied, and it will be possible to assign a class to the sample. <a href="#tbl4" class="usa-link">Table <a href="#tbl4" class="usa-link">4</a></a> shows the numbers
of PCs chosen for each model and the samples used in the training
and validation steps.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="tbl4"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 4. Characteristics of
the SIMCA Models.</h4>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/tbl4/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="t4fn1">
<sup>a</sup><p class="display-inline">For the validation,
all of the samples
analyzed were employed.</p>
</div></div></section><p>Class boundaries were established for each predefined target class
model on the basis of the values of Hotelling’s <em>T</em><sup>2</sup> and residual <em>Q</em> statistics. The classification
criteria of the samples regarding each region were defined using a
combination of the reduced <em>T</em><sup>2</sup> and <em>Q</em> statistic values. Thus, for a sample to be considered
as belonging to a certain target class, both values must be less than
1.0.</p>
<p>Because the number of available samples from each Italian
region
was limited, each single-class model training was carried out employing
all of the samples belonging to the concerned target class. Then,
all 73 samples, both those belonging to the target class and those
not, were used for validation purposes. All of the samples were correctly
classified in five of the six single-class models, and the quality
parameters such as sensitivity, specificity, precision, efficiency
(accuracy), and area under the receiver operating characteristic curve
(AUC) were equal to 1.00.<sup><a href="#ref42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref42">42</a></sup> The only model
that misclassified one of the samples was the Garda model, in which
a Garda sample was considered as non-Garda. Thus, in this model, the
sensitivity, specificity, precision, efficiency (accuracy), and AUC
were equal to 0.90, 1.00, 1.00, 0.99, and 0.95, respectively. The
classification plots of each model are shown in the supplementary
material (Supporting Information <a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.jafc.1c02981/suppl_file/jf1c02981_si_001.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Figures S3–S8</a>).</p></section><section id="sec3.7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Regional Signatures and GC × GC Identitation Potential</h3>
<p>Based on the information shown in <a href="#tbl3" class="usa-link">Table <a href="#tbl3" class="usa-link">3</a></a>, it is possible to derive some conclusions
about peculiar chemical traits specific to certain regions. For example,
compounds #28 (<em>n</em>-hexane), #109 (1-penten-3-ol), and
#386, #475, and #510 (all unidentified) are characteristic of the
Garda region. In the same way, compound #141 ((<em>E</em>,<em>E</em>)-2,4-hexadienal) is specific of Sicilia samples, compound
#95 (2-ethyl-2-hexenal) of Lazio, and compound #27 (<em>n</em>-octane) of Umbria. Further assignments could be identified as characteristic
of more than one region, e.g., compound #245 (unidentified) is associated
with Garda and Umbria. In the same way, following this assignment
methodology, and considering the presence/absence of a few volatile
compounds previously selected, a classification tree rule could be
deduced to classify undoubtedly any sample of EVOO from any of the
six considered regions. However, it might be beneficial to have a
larger set of representative olive oil samples from each of the regions
for such a classification tree to be sufficiently reliable.</p>
<p>The classification strategy proposed in this study is based on using
the whole UT fingerprint of volatiles that is established by considering
simultaneously all of the compounds that have been selected as characteristic
of at least one of the regions concerned. In this way, the one-class
SIMCA classification models are applied sequentially to any EVOO sample,
regardless of geographic origin, so that the oil is assigned to one
of the regions. This overall classification approach based on the
use of UT fingerprinting, i.e., identitation, overcomes the main drawback
for the routine application of single-step multivariate models.</p>
<p>Moreover, the strategy takes full advantage of the high-resolution
power of GC × GC that effectively maps all detectable volatile
components including (a) those related to major functional variables
(e.g., olive cultivar,<sup><a href="#ref13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref13">13</a></sup> olive ripening
stage,<sup><a href="#ref16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref16">16</a></sup> harvest year, and processing technology<sup><a href="#ref12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref12">12</a></sup>), here playing a confounding role in regional
classification; and (b) several potent odorants delineating EVOO sensory
features. The latter might be masked by coelution phenomena occurring
in 1D-GC<sup><a href="#ref18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref18">18</a></sup> while resulting in less effective
identitation and poorly informative profiling processes.</p></section></section><section id="notes-1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Supporting Information Available</h2>
